## Page 1


Analytic index theory and spectral flow in real Hilbert C∗-modules
Chris Bourne†, ♠, Alan Carey‡, Koen van den Dungen§, Adam Rennie∗
†Institute of Liberal Arts and Sciences, Graduate School of Mathematics,
Nagoya University, Furo-cho, Chikusa-ku, Nagoya, 464-8601, Japan
♠RIKEN iTHEMS, 2-1 Hirosawa, Wako, Saitama 351-0198, Japan
‡Mathematical Sciences Institute, Australian National University,
Canberra ACT, 0200, Australia
§Mathematisches Institut, Universit¨at Bonn,
Endenicher Allee 60, D-53115 Bonn, Germany
∗School of Mathematics and Physics, University of Wollongong,
Wollongong, 2522, Australia
1 July 2026
Abstract
We consider the analytic index and spectral flow of Fredholm operators on Hilbert C∗-
modules. Our spaces and algebras are equipped with a real structure, so the analytic index and
spectral flow takes value in the real K-theory group of a σ-unital C∗-algebra. We use Van Daele
K-theory, which allows us to treat the eight real K-theory groups and the two complex groups
on an equal footing. We provide a general definition of the analytic index for Clifford anti-
linear and skew-adjoint Fredholm operators as well as self-adjoint and odd Fredholm operators.
Our definition of spectral flow and its basic properties are valid for Wahl-continuous paths of
Fredholm operators on a real Hilbert C∗-module. We also provide an analytic approach to the
spectral flow as a decomposition into a finite sum of relative indices. Furthermore, we prove a
real version of the Robbin–Salamon theorem, relating the spectral flow to a Fredholm index. Our
description of the index and spectral flow relies on various isomorphisms between Kasparov’s
KKR-theory and Van Daele K-theory, which we systematically describe in the Appendix.
Keywords: Index theory, spectral flow, Hilbert C∗-modules, Clifford algebra, K-theory, KK-theory
AMS Subject Classification: 19K56, 19K35, 46L80

Introduction
The celebrated paper of Atiyah and Singer [6] provides a comprehensive study on the homotopy
theory of the space of skew-adjoint Fredholm operators on real or complex Hilbert spaces which
are Cℓ0,k-anti-linear (i.e., they anti-commute with the generators of an ungraded Clifford algebra
Cℓ0,k represented on the Hilbert space). The skew-adjoint Fredholm index then labels the connected
components of the space of such Fredholm operators. See [7, 46] for applications to index theory
and geometry. Motivated by recent applications to topological phases of matter [1, 22], the paper
[16] (by some of the authors and Matthias Lesch) considered the fundamental group and spectral
flow of Cℓ0,k-anti-linear skew-adjoint Fredholm operators on real Hilbert spaces.
For Fredholm operators on complex spaces, the extension of index theory and spectral flow from
Hilbert spaces to Hilbert C∗-modules has already been extensively studied (for a non-exhaustive
list, see e.g. [48, 59, 34, 47, 66, 20, 21, 45, 18, 30, 62, 26, 49, 50]). In this paper, it is our goal to
present a general Fredholm theory on real Hilbert C∗-modules. We will provide a comprehensive
description of both the Fredholm index and the spectral flow for Cℓ0,k-anti-linear skew-adjoint
Fredholm operators on real Hilbert C∗-modules over some real C∗-algebra A.
The skew-adjoint Fredholm index of Atiyah and Singer, defined for Cℓ0,k−1-anti-linear skew-adjoint
Fredholm operators on a real Hilbert space, takes values in the real K-theory group KO−k of a
point. An elegant description of this index can be obtained via the Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro (ABS)
isomorphism [4]. Indeed, the kernel Ker(F) of the Fredholm operator F is a finite-dimensional
Cℓ0,k−1-module, and thus defines an element in the Grothendieck group M0,k−1 of (ungraded) finite-
dimensional representations of Cℓ0,k−1. The equivalence class of Ker(F) (modulo Cℓ0,k-modules)
then provides an element in M0,k−1/M0,k. Under the ABS isomorphism M0,k−1/M0,k ∼= KO−k, the
equivalence class of Ker(F) then corresponds to the Fredholm index of F in KO−k.
Similarly, the skew-adjoint spectral flow from [16] can also be described explicitly in terms of
the Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro picture of real K-theory, and it generalises both the ordinary Z-valued
spectral flow as well as the Z2-valued spectral flow [19, 22].
In order to generalise the Fredholm theory from real Hilbert spaces to real Hilbert C∗-modules,
it might then be tempting to first try to generalise the ABS isomorphism from Hilbert spaces to
C∗-modules.
Unfortunately, the analogous ABS construction for C∗-modules (simply replacing
finite-dimensional vector spaces by finite projective C∗-modules over A) does not agree with the
real K-theory group KOk(A). For the record, we provide a simple counterexample in Appendix
A. In the C∗-module setting, we therefore need to move away from the ABS picture, and take a
different approach.
First, instead of real spaces, we will work with Real spaces (with capitalised R), which are com-
plex spaces equipped with real structures (one may move between the real and Real pictures by

complexifying or restricting to elements invariant under the real structure), and consequently we
replace real KO-groups by Real KR-groups. In analogy to [6, 16], we then consider Cℓr,s-anti-linear
skew-adjoint Fredholm operators on a Real Hilbert C∗-module over a Real C∗-algebra A. We will
show that such Fredholm operators naturally define an element in Kasparov’s Real KK-theory
group KKR(Cℓr,s, A) (for a brief introduction to Real KK-theory, we refer to Appendix B).
We would like to define the Fredholm index and spectral flow taking values in the Real K-theory
groups KRk(A) (where k depends on the choice of r, s). These K-theory groups are defined in
terms of the k-fold suspension of A as KRk(A) := KR0(SkA). However, we will find it much more
convenient to avoid these suspensions and instead use the already present Clifford algebra Cℓr,s to
label the degree of the K-theory group. To be able to do this, we need a description of K-theory
which allows for Z2-graded C∗-algebras. Our receptacle for the Fredholm index and spectral flow
will therefore be Van Daele’s K-theory groups [60, 61], denoted DK(A) (for an overview of the
definition and properties of Van Daele K-theory, we refer to Appendix C). For an ungraded C∗-
algebra A, the Van Daele K-theory groups are related to the (usual) Real K-theory groups via the
isomorphisms Υr+1,s

Our description of the Fredholm index and spectral flow in Van Daele K-theory will also rely
heavily on various isomorphisms between Van Daele K-theory and Kasparov’s Real KK-theory
developed in [55, 42, 17]. Adapting these isomorphisms to the setting with Clifford symmetries
requires some care.
One reason is that KK-theory is bivariant and we have the isomorphism
Σr,s

is more subtle (see §2.3). We will provide a detailed study of these Clifford-decorated isomorphisms
between KKR-theory and DK-theory in Appendix D. In particular, we carefully show that these
isomorphisms are compatible with the notions of Bott periodicity, Morita invariance, and other
maps within KK-theory and DK-theory.
Throughout this paper, we will be explicit by including the notation for all isomorphisms in and
between KK-theory and DK-theory. Although this makes the notation somewhat more cumber-
some, we believe this may help the reader to better understand our definitions and constructions.
Moreover, whenever possible, we will always work with concrete isomorphisms applied to expli-
cit representatives of equivalence classes, ensuring that our constructions can be useful in explicit
computations.
Let us now provide a brief overview of the main contents of this paper.
The Fredholm index
The well-known theory of (bounded or unbounded) Fredholm operators
on Hilbert C∗-modules is adapted from the complex setting to the Real setting in §2. We show
that a Cℓr,s-anti-linear skew-adjoint Fredholm operator yields a well-defined class in KKR-theory,
and establish the technical preliminaries to systematically incorporate Clifford symmetries in both
KKR- and DK-theory.
In §3, we then provide our definition of the Fredholm index, which is defined for (bounded or
unbounded) Cℓr,s-anti-linear skew-adjoint Fredholm operators, and takes values in the Van Daele
K-theory group DK(A ⊗Cℓr+1,s+1). We show that the Fredholm index factors through the KKR-
class of the Fredholm operator and the isomorphism between KKR-theory and DK-theory. Thus,
in a generalised sense, we can understand the Fredholm index on Real C∗-modules as providing the
link between KKR-theory and Van Daele K-theory. Furthermore, we show that our Fredholm index
agrees with the (even or odd) index on complex C∗-modules [62], as well as with the skew-adjoint
Fredholm index on real Hilbert spaces via the ABS isomorphism [16].


## Page 5


analytic index theory and spectral flow in real hilbert c∗-modules
5
The relative index
§4 first gives a presentation of (relative) Van Daele K-theory in terms of
Cℓr,s-anti-linear skew-adjoint unitaries (see also [1, §3.3]). We use this presentation in §5 to define
the relative index for a Fredholm pair of such skew-adjoint unitaries. Again, we show that the
relative index factors through a KKR-class constructed from the Fredholm pair. Moreover, we
show that our DK-valued relative index agrees with the (even or odd) relative index on complex
C∗-modules, as well as with the relative index on real Hilbert spaces considered in [16].
The spectral flow
In order to introduce the spectral flow of a path of Fredholm operators, we
first study families of Fredholm operators in §6. Given a compact space Ωand a family {Dω}ω∈Ω
of (densely-defined, self-adjoint or skew-adjoint) regular Fredholm operators on a C∗-module YB,
we can consider the family operator D• on C(Ω, YB)C(Ω,B). To study such families, we describe the
Wahl topology on C∗-modules. This topology was introduced by Wahl for (unbounded) Fredholm
operators on Hilbert spaces [63]. It is weaker than the gap topology, but still strong enough to be
suitable for Fredholm theory. We show that D• is regular and Fredholm if and only if the family
{Dω}ω∈Ωis Wahl-continuous. Thus, the Wahl topology provides the appropriate topology for the
study of families of Fredholm operators, and in fact it is the weakest topology under which the
spectral flow can be defined.
In §7, we then provide the (abstract) definition of the DK-valued spectral flow for a Wahl-continuous
path of Cℓr,s-anti-linear skew-adjoint Fredholm operators with invertible endpoints, by simply com-
bining our DK-valued Fredholm index with Bott periodicity.
We prove that the spectral flow
satisfies the properties of homotopy invariance, additivity under direct sum, additivity under con-
catenation of paths, as well as a normalisation property relating the spectral flow to the relative
index of a Fredholm pair of skew-adjoint unitaries. We also show that our spectral flow agrees with
the (even or odd) spectral flow on complex C∗-modules [62].
In §8, we provide an analytic approach to our spectral flow, in analogy to the work of J. Phillips
on complex Hilbert spaces [51, 52]. The latter is constructed from a finite partition of the path of
Fredholm operators and yields a finite sum of relative indices of projections. In our Real setting,
we will replace the relative index of projections by our DK-valued relative index of skew-adjoint
unitaries (as in [16]). In fact, we provide two versions of the analytic spectral flow. First, our
Phillips formula is analogous to [52] and works under the assumption that the path of Fredholm
operators is Riesz-continuous. Second, our Wahl formula is an adaptation of the approach by Wahl
[62] on complex C∗-modules, which works for Wahl-continuous paths, but requires the assumption
of locally trivialising families. Our Phillips formula also allows us to prove that the DK-valued
spectral flow agrees with the skew-adjoint spectral flow on real Hilbert spaces from [16].
‘Index = spectral flow’
The close relation between the Fredholm index and the spectral flow is
well-known and has been used already in the work of Atiyah–Patodi–Singer [5, §7]. The Robbin–
Salamon Theorem [54] shows that the spectral flow of a family of (unbounded) self-adjoint Fredholm
operators {Dt} on a complex Hilbert space equals the Fredholm index of the operator ∂t + D•. In
§9, we prove a generalisation of the Robbin–Salamon Theorem in the setting of Real C∗-modules,
which relates the DK-valued spectral flow of a path {Tt}t∈[0,1] to the DK-valued Fredholm index
of an operator (T• + ∂t)+.
Examples from physics
One of our motivations for the present paper was the discovery of
families of Hamiltonians in condensed matter physics that fail to be gap continuous but are Wahl-


## Page 6


6
C. Bourne, A. Carey, K. van den Dungen, A. Rennie
continuous [58, §4.5.1]. Furthermore, our setting of Cℓr,s-anti-linear skew-adjoint Fredholm op-
erators is a natural framework for the study of Hamiltonians with free fermionic symmetries as
investigated by Zirnbauer et al. [41, 1]. In §10, we then consider some applications of our DK-
valued spectral flow to the study of topological phases of matter. By introducing an auxiliary
algebra of observables A, we have an interpretation of a relative index of gapped Hamiltonians
as a spectral flow taking values in the Van Daele K-theory of A. Hamiltonians on (discrete) half
spaces that are translation invariant parallel to the boundary also give rise to loops of Fredholm
operators on a C∗-module over an algebra describing the boundary, which also has a spectral flow
description. In many cases of interest, this spectral flow can be directly related to the boundary
map of a boundary-free (bulk) system and so provides a general spectral flow interpretation of the
bulk-boundary correspondence.
Appendix
As mentioned above, we explain in Appendix A that the naive extension of the ABS
isomorphism to Real Hilbert C∗-modules does not hold. The mechanisms that underpin our results
are Real Kasparov theory, Van Daele K-theory, and how these two theories are related. Appendices
B, C, and D, respectively review KKR-theory, DK-theory, and their compatibility with each other.
Appendix E gives a summary of the maps used throughout the paper (see also the Notation and
conventions section below).

Notation and conventions
• All linear spaces (e.g., Hilbert spaces, C∗-algebras, Hilbert C∗-modules) are Real, which means
they are complex and come equipped with a real structure r, an anti-linear involution. The
fixed points under this action will define a linear space over R. When necessary, we write rY
to denote a real structure on the space Y . We say y ∈Y is Real if y = yr. The real structure
on a C∗-algebra A satisfies (ab)r = arbr for all a, b ∈A.
• All C∗-algebras will be σ-unital. We will consider both Z2-graded and ungraded C∗-algebras,
where B generally denotes a (possibly) Z2-graded C∗-algebra, and A an ungraded C∗-algebra.
If B is Z2-graded, then B = B0 ⊕B1, BiBj ⊂Bi+j mod 2, and b ∈B is called even (resp.
odd) if b ∈B0 (resp. B1). A graded ∗-homomorphism ϕ : B →C of Z2-graded C∗-algebras
satisfies ϕ(Bi) ⊂Ci, i = 0, 1. We also write deg(b) = i if b ∈Bi. The multiplier algebra of B
is denoted Mult(B), and we denote the Calkin algebra of B by QB := Mult(B)/B.
• The Z2-graded tensor product of the Z2-graded C∗-algebras B, C is the usual (spatial) tensor

For the convenience of the reader, Appendix E provides a list with our notation of various iso-

2
Fredholm operators on Real Hilbert modules
In this section, we consider Fredholm operators on (possibly Z2-graded) Real C∗-modules YB and
their connection to Kasparov’s Real KKR-theory. For a brief review of KKR-theory, we refer to
Appendix B.


## Page 9


analytic index theory and spectral flow in real hilbert c∗-modules
9
In the complex setting, bounded Fredholm operators on complex C∗-modules were studied, in all
generality, by Exel [27] (see also [28, §4]). In the real setting, real Fredholm operators with closed
range were described by Schr¨oder [57]. In this section, we adapt the approach of Wahl [62] on
complex C∗-modules to study unbounded Fredholm operators on Real C∗-modules.
2.1
Fredholm operators on Hilbert modules
Let YB be a countably generated and (possibly) Z2-graded C∗-module over a (possibly Z2-graded)
C∗-algebra B.
Definition 2.1. An adjointable operator T : Y 1
B →Y 2
B between Hilbert B-modules is called
Fredholm if there exists an adjointable operator S : Y 2
B →Y 1
B such that TS −1Y 2 ∈End0
B(Y 2) and
ST −1Y 1 ∈End0
B(Y 1). For T ∈EndB(Y ), this means that T is Fredholm if qY (T) ∈QB(Y ) =
EndB(Y )/ End0
B(Y ) is invertible.
A (densely-defined) regular operator D ∈RegB(Y ) is called Fredholm if the bounded transform
FD := D(1 + D∗D)−1/2 ∈EndB(Y ) is Fredholm.
Following Wahl [62], we recall below that any self-adjoint regular Fredholm operator gives rise to a
(bounded) Kasparov C-B-module and hence to a class in KK(C, B), and we will explain how this
approach can be adapted to skew-adjoint operators as well.
Proposition 2.2 ([62, Proposition 2.1, Corollary 2.2]). For a self-adjoint operator D ∈RegB(Y ),
the following statements are equivalent:
(i) D is Fredholm.
(ii) There is an ε > 0 such that for all φ ∈Cc(−ε, ε), φ(D) ∈End0
B(Y ).
(iii) There is an ε > 0 such that for any continuous function χ : R →R such that χ|(−∞,−ε] = −1
and χ|[ε,∞) = +1, χ(D)2 −1 ∈End0
B(Y ).
Analogously, for a skew-adjoint operator T ∈RegB(Y ), the following statements are equivalent:
(i’) T is Fredholm.
(ii’) There is an ε > 0 such that for all φ ∈Cc(−iε, iε), φ(T) ∈End0
B(Y ).
(iii’) There is an ε > 0 such that for any continuous function χ : iR →iR such that χ|(−i∞,−iε] = −i
and χ|[iε,i∞) = +i, χ(T)2 + 1 ∈End0
B(Y ).
Definition 2.3. Let D ∈RegB(Y ) be self-adjoint. We say that a smooth odd non-decreasing
function χ : R →R is a normalising function for D if χ′(0) > 0, limx→∞χ(x) = 1 and χ(D)2 −1 ∈
End0
B(Y ).
If T ∈RegB(Y ) is skew-adjoint, then a smooth odd non-decreasing function χ : iR →iR is a
normalising function for T if χ is strictly increasing in a neighbourhood of 0, limix→∞χ(ix) = i
and χ(T)2 + 1 ∈End0
B(Y ).
Remark 2.4. Normalising functions are guaranteed to exist for Fredholm operators by Proposition
2.2. Furthermore, if YB is a Real C∗-module and T = T r, then χ(T)r = χ(T).

2.2
Clifford anti-linear Fredholm operators
Proposition 2.6 shows that an odd Real self-adjoint Fredholm operator defines a class in the Real K-
theory group KKR(C, B) ∼= KR0(B). We shall more generally be interested in describing elements
of the higher KR-theory groups KKR(Cℓr,s, B) ∼= KRr−s(B) in terms of Fredholm operators. For
this purpose, we consider Fredholm operators which anti-commute with a given representation of
the Clifford algebra Cℓr,s. In the Real setting, it will be more convenient to work with skew-adjoint
(instead of self-adjoint) operators.
So let XA be a full countably generated and ungraded Real Hilbert C∗-module over an un-
graded Real C∗-algebra A.

Index theory on Real Hilbert modules
In this section, we will define the Fredholm index of a Real skew-adjoint Fredholm operator, taking
values in Van Daele K-theory (or DK-theory, for short). An introduction to Van Daele K-theory
is given in Appendix C, and various concrete isomorphisms between Kasparov’s Real KKR-theory
and Van Daele’s DK-theory are presented in Appendix D. As in §2.2, we are mainly interested
in Real skew-adjoint Fredholm operators which anti-commute with the generators of a Clifford
representation.
3.1
The complex analytic index in DK-theory
We first recall the construction of the Fredholm index in complex K-theory.
For a Fredholm
operator on a complex Hilbert space, one defines Index0(T) := dim Ker(T) −dim Coker(T) ∈Z.
Using the identification K0(C) ≃Z, we may rewrite the index as a formal difference of projections
Index0(T) :=

PKer(T)

−

PCoker(T)

∈K0(C).
Generalising the index to complex C∗-modules is complicated by the fact that the kernel and
cokernel of a Fredholm operator on a C∗-module might fail to be complemented (in which case, the
projections onto the kernel or cokernel do not exist).
Even index
The work of Mingo [47] and Exel [27] shows that, given an adjointable Fredholm
operator T on a C∗-module XA, there exists a suitable compact perturbation eT of T on XA ⊕An
for some n such that eT has closed range. Having closed range ensures that the kernel and cokernel
of eT are complemented, and one may then define the Fredholm index as
Index0(T) :=

PKer( eT)

−



∈K0(A),
(3.1)
which turns out to be independent of the choice of perturbation ˜T and so is well-defined.

3.2
The real analytic index
We now consider the Real setting, which allows for eight different indices taking values in the
Real K-theory groups KRp(A) (p = 0, . . . , 7). In order to accommodate these eight versions of
the Fredholm index, we will consider Fredholm operators which anti-commute with a Clifford
representation, and we will replace KR-theory by Van Daele K-theory (DK-theory) as the receptacle
for our Fredholm index. Due to the impossibility of extending the ABS isomorphism to C∗-modules
(cf. Appendix A), the index is defined in terms of boundary maps.

The index, stabilisation, and KKR-theory
Recall from Corollary 2.7 that any Real skew-adjoint Fredholm operator T ∈Regr,s
A (X) also gives

First we must address the problem that two such Fredholm operators T and T ′ may be defined on
different C∗-modules. In particular q(T) and q(T ′) will lie in different Calkin algebras, and we will
only be able to make meaningful comparison after applying boundary and Morita maps. To get
around this issue, we use Kasparov’s stabilisation theorem in order to compare operators on the
standard C∗-module HA.

T

The map is surjective as any odd self-adjoint unitary x ∈QB ˆ⊗K will define a Kasparov module
(C, ˆH ˆ⊗B, ˜x) with ˜x ∈Mult(B ˆ⊗K) a lift of x. For injectivity, any trivial odd self-adjoint unitary
x ∈QB ˆ⊗K will be stably homotopic to an odd self-adjoint unitary x0 such that there exists a lift
˜x0 ∈Mult(B ˆ⊗K) which is also an odd self-adjoint unitary. Such a homotopy will then induce a
homotopy from (C, ˆH ˆ⊗B, ˜x) to (C, ˆH ˆ⊗B, ˜x0), a degenerate Kasparov module.

Since the index factors through KKR, we can immediately conclude its basic stability properties. To
consider homotopy invariance of the index, we use the Wahl topology of operators on C∗-modules,
which is introduced in §6 below (see Corollary 6.8 in particular).

A Z2-graded index

shuffle maps that appear in the Clifford anti-linear setting. The proof of the following is analogous
to Theorem 3.11.

3.5
Comparison with Real Hilbert spaces
Let us fix a separable Real Hilbert space H with an ample Cℓr,s-representation in B(H) generated
by {e1, . . . , er, f1, . . . , fs}. We can always add more Clifford generators by considering H⊕2 with
er+1 = σ1 and fs+1 = −iσ2. We will therefore assume that r, s ≥1. The set Regr,s(H) denotes
the closed operators on H that anti-commute with the Clifford generators.
In [6], Atiyah and
Singer studied the index theory of the space of skew-adjoint Fredhom operators in Reg0,k(H).1 If
T ∈Regr,s(H) is skew-adjoint and Fredholm, then Ker(T) is a (finite-dimensional) Cℓr,s-module
and we can use the Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro isomorphism [4] to define

In this section, we will introduce an analogous definition for the relative index on Real C∗-modules,
replacing projections by skew-adjoint unitaries, generalising the relative index defined on real Hil-
bert space introduced in [16, §4]. We show that our relative index agrees with the case of complex
C∗-modules as described by Wahl [62, §3 & §8] and Ng–Sutradhar–Wang [50]. Similarly to the con-
struction of the analytic spectral flow by Phillips [52], we will use our relative index of skew-adjoint
unitaries to define the analytic spectral flow on Real C∗-modules in §8.

Families of Fredholm operators
As in §2, YB is a (possibly Z2-graded) countably generated C∗-module over a (possibly Z2-graded)
C∗-algebra.
In this section, we will consider families of operators parametrised by a compact
Hausdorff space Ω. If {Sω}ω∈Ωis such a family of operators on YB, then we define the family
operator S• on C(Ω, YB)C(Ω,B) by (S•y)(ω) := Sωy(ω) for y ∈C(Ω, YB). To ensure that S• is
a well-defined adjointable/compact operator (in the bounded case) or regular operator (in the
unbounded case), we need the family {Sω}ω∈Ωto be suitably continuous, in the following precise
sense.

6.1
The Wahl topology on a Hilbert module
In §7, we will define the spectral flow for a family of Real skew-adjoint Fredholm operators
{Tt}t∈[0,1] ⊂Regr,s
A (X) with invertible endpoints. For this purpose, we will need the family to
be “sufficiently continuous”, such that the family operator T• ∈Regr,s
SA(SX) is also regular and
Fredholm (note that T• is automatically Real and skew-adjoint). We have already seen in Lemma
6.1 that the regularity of T• requires the family {Tt}t∈[0,1] to have strongly continuous resolvents.
The Fredholmness of T• additionally requires that we can find a “suitably continuous” parametrix
Q• = {Qt}t∈[0,1] for T•. We will see that, to obtain the Fredholm property, the appropriate notion


## Page 36


36
C. Bourne, A. Carey, K. van den Dungen, A. Rennie
of continuity is given by the Wahl topology. This topology was introduced by Wahl [63] for self-
adjoint Fredholm operators on Hilbert spaces. As remarked in [63], this topology and its basic
properties can be naturally extended to operators on Hilbert C∗-modules. Here, we will review the
construction of the Wahl topology for both self-adjoint and skew-adjoint Fredholm operators on
C∗-modules. (If YB is a Real C∗-module, we will silently assume all operators to be Real as well.)
Consider a family of regular self-adjoint (resp. skew-adjoint) Fredholm operators {Dω}ω∈Ω, such
that the resolvents are strongly continuous (cf. Lemma 6.1). From Proposition 2.2 we know that,
for each ω ∈Ω, there is an ε > 0 such that for all φ ∈Cc(−ε, ε) (resp. φ ∈Cc(−iε, iε)), φ(Dω)
is compact. The basic idea of the Wahl topology is then to ensure that we can choose such an ε
uniformly, such that ω 7→φ(Dω) is norm-continuous.

Theorem 6.6. Let Ωbe a compact Hausdorff space and let {Dω}ω∈Ωbe a family of regular self-
adjoint (or skew-adjoint) Fredholm operators on YB. Then the following are equivalent:
(i) The family {Dω}ω∈Ωis Wahl-continuous.
(ii) The family operator D• : Dom(D•) →C(Ω, YB)C(Ω,B) is regular and Fredholm.
Proof. Assume {Dω}ω∈Ωis Wahl-continuous. Since Wahl-continuous families have strongly con-
tinuous resolvents, the regularity of D• is given by Lemma 6.1. Furthermore, there exists an n ∈N
such that ω 7→ϕn(Dω) ∈Fsa
n (YB) (resp. ∈Fsk
n (YB)) is compact and norm-continuous. In particular,
ϕn(D•) is compact, and it follows from Proposition 2.2 that D• is Fredholm.
Conversely, assume D• is regular and Fredholm. Then the regularity implies the strong continuity
of the resolvents (Lemma 6.1). Furthermore, by Proposition 2.2, the Fredholm property of D•
implies that there exists an n ∈N such that ϕn(D•) is compact, which means that ϕn(Dω) is
compact and depends norm-continuously on ω. Thus {Dω}ω∈Ωis Wahl-continuous.
For our construction of the spectral flow for a path of Fredholm operators (i.e., for Ω= [0, 1]) in
§7, we will need to consider paths with invertible endpoints, in which case we have:
Corollary 6.7. Let {Dt}t∈[0,1] be a family of regular self-adjoint (or skew-adjoint) Fredholm oper-
ators on YB. Then the following are equivalent:
(i) D0 and D1 are invertible, and the family {Dt}t∈[0,1] is Wahl-continuous.
(ii) The family operator D• : Dom(D•) →SYSB is regular and Fredholm.
Proof. We need to adapt the equivalent statements of Theorem 6.6 from the compact space Ω=
[0, 1] to the noncompact space (0, 1). Assuming Wahl-continuity of {Dt}t∈[0,1] and invertibility of
D0 and D1, we can now choose n ∈N large enough, such that ω 7→ϕn(Dω) is compact and norm-
continuous with ϕn(D0) = ϕn(D1) = 0. This means that ϕn(D•) is compact on SYSB. Conversely,
if ϕn(D•) is compact on SYSB, then this in particular means ϕn(D0) = ϕn(D1) = 0, which shows
that D0 and D1 are invertible.
For completeness, we also examine Wahl-continuous families of Fredholm operators at the level of
Kasparov modules and KK-theory.
Corollary 6.8. Let Ωbe a compact Hausdorff space, YB a Z2-graded Hilbert module and D : Ω→

can be found in the book [23].
7
Spectral flow on Real Hilbert modules
In this section, we will introduce the spectral flow for paths of skew-adjoint Fredholm operators
on Real Hilbert C∗-modules with invertible endpoints. We provide an abstract definition of DK-
valued spectral flow by simply combining our DK-valued Fredholm index with Bott periodicity. By
observing that (like the Fredholm index) the spectral flow factors through KK-theory, we derive
several elementary properties of the spectral flow (such as additivity under concatenation of paths).
We also prove a normalisation property for the spectral flow: given a Fredholm pair of skew-adjoint
unitaries (J0, J1), the spectral flow of the straight line path from J0 to J1 equals the (inverse of the)
relative index from §5. Finally, in §7.4, we compare our DK-valued spectral flow with the (even or
odd) spectral flow in complex K-theory as described by Wahl [62].
7.1
Abstract definition
As always, let XA be a full ungraded and countably generated C∗-module with an ungraded and
ample Cℓr,s-representation generated by {e1, . . . , er, f1, . . . fs}. We aim to define the spectral flow for

and Fredholm. As we have seen in Corollary 6.7, this is equivalent to the requirements that the

To obtain an abstract definition of the spectral flow of a path {Tt}t∈[0,1] ⊂Regr,s

8
Analytic spectral flow
In this section, we provide a more concrete description of spectral flow that is closer in spirit to
the idea of counting eigenvalue crossings through zero for paths of Fredholm operators. First, in
§8.1, we show that the normalisation property of the spectral flow (relating the spectral flow of the
straight line path between two skew-adjoint unitaries to their relative index, see Proposition 7.4)
can be generalised to norm-continuous paths of “small perturbations”.
In §8.2, we then prove a formula for the analytic spectral flow in analogy to the analytic approach
to spectral flow on complex Hilbert spaces due to J. Phillips [52]. This analytic spectral flow of
a path {Dt}t∈[0,1] of self-adjoint Fredholm operators is given by considering a partition 0 = t0 <
t1, . . . < tn = 1 of the unit interval, taking the positive spectral projections P+
 χ(Dti)

, and then
computing a (finite) sum of relative indices of these projections.
In the C∗-module setting, the construction of this analytic spectral flow is complicated by the
absence of Borel functional calculus on C∗-modules (in general, positive spectral projections can
not always be constructed via continuous functional calculus). In §8.2, we deal with this issue
by considering only Riesz-continuous paths. In our Real setting, we replace projections by skew-
adjoint unitaries, and define the analytic spectral flow in analogy with [52]. We prove that this
analytic spectral flow agrees with the (abstract) spectral flow of §7. In the special setting of a
Hilbert space, our analytic formula for the spectral flow shows that the DK-valued spectral flow
reduces to the (inverse of the) skew-adjoint spectral flow on real Hilbert spaces considered in [16]
(see §8.3).
In §8.4, we adapt the approach of Wahl [62] to our Real setting, and prove a second formula for the
analytic spectral flow. Wahl’s approach works for Wahl-continuous families of regular (unbounded)
Fredholm operators, but requires the additional assumption that there exist locally trivialising
families.

to J1 is given by the relative index of (J1, J0). We can generalise this result as follows: instead of

8.3
Comparison with real Hilbert spaces
Using our analytic description of the spectral flow, we can now relate our DK-valued spectral
flow to the spectral flow on real Hilbert spaces considered in [16, §5-6]. Recall also from §5.5 the
description of the relative index on real Hilbert spaces in terms of Clifford modules from [16, §4].
As discussed in Remark 7.5, our DK-valued spectral flow has a normalisation that is inconsistent
with the Clifford module valued spectral flow from [16]. So while there is a global minus sign, this
only affects the case where the range is KO0(R) or KO4(R).

Proof. Since the path {χ(Tt)}t∈[0,1] is norm-continuous, we know from Lemma 8.2 that, for each
t0 ∈[0, 1], there exists a skew-adjoint unitary Jt0 such that Bt0 := Jt0 −χ(Tt0) is compact. By
norm-continuity, it follows that χ(Tt) + Bt0 is invertible on some compact neighbourhood Ω0 of t0,
so that the constant family {Bt0}t∈Ω0 provides a trivialising family on this neighbourhood. Hence
both Definitions 8.3 and 8.9 of the analytic spectral flow apply (where the latter is applied to
{χ(Tt)}), and they are equal by Theorems 8.5 and 8.11.

The Robbin–Salamon theorem
The Robbin–Salamon theorem [54] relates the spectral flow of a family of (unbounded) self-adjoint
Fredholm operators {Dt} on a complex Hilbert space to the Fredholm index of the operator ∂t+D•.
Many generalisations of this result have been investigated, particularly from the viewpoint of the
Kasparov product in unbounded KK-theory [32, 24, 16, 25, 26]. In this section, we will present a
similar result in the setting of Real C∗-modules.

10
Examples from physics
10.1
Free fermion systems with Altland–Zirnbauer symmetries
We follow a description of free fermionic Hamiltonians and ground states used by Zirnbauer et
al. [41, 1], which is based on the Hartree–Fock–Bogoliubov mean-field approximation.
We are
typically interested in models of (superconducting) electrons, more generally fermions, which we
model mathematically via vectors in a complex Hilbert space V. The anti-particles (electron holes)
are modelled via the dual space V∗. Key properties of superconducting systems can be approximated
via the dynamics of a self-adjoint Hamiltonian H on the Hilbert space H = V ⊕V∗. The complex
Hilbert space H is a Real Hilbert space via the real structure ψr = Γψ, where Γ =
0
R−1
R
0

is
a self-adjoint antiunitary and R : V →V∗is the Riesz isomorphism. We therefore obtain a real
structure on B(H) via AdΓ.
The dynamics of a physical system is modelled by a Hamiltonian H = H∗on H. In particular, H
gives rise to a time evolution unitary Ut = eitH, which should be invariant under the real structure Γ.
The relation ΓUtΓ = Ut for all t is equivalent to the relation ΓHΓ = −H. We call any Hamiltonian
satisfying ΓHΓ = −H a Bogoliubov–de Gennes (BdG) Hamiltonian. A BdG Hamiltonian then
induces a dynamics on the anti-symmetric Fock space V∗V, which gives a mathematical description
of the many-body system and ground state. Because the dynamics on V∗V are determined by H
on H, the model is a free fermionic approximation of many-body phenomena.
Altland and Zirnbauer described fundamental symmetries of such free fermionic systems [2], which
are described by unitary or anti-unitary operators that commute with the BdG Hamiltonian H. A
simple mathematical description of these symmetries was then noted by Kennedy and Zirnbauer.


## Page 55


analytic index theory and spectral flow in real hilbert c∗-modules
55
Proposition 10.1 ([41, §2], [1, §3.1–3.2]). Let H be a BdG Hamiltonian on (H, Γ). If H has
Altland–Zirnbauer symmetries, then there are Real mutually anti-commuting skew-adjoint unitaries
{κj}n
j=1 ⊂B(H), such that κjiH = −iHκj for all j = 1, . . . , n. The integer n ∈{0, . . . , 7} is
determined by the free fermionic symmetry.
There is considerable mathematical and physical interest in studying the topological properties of
many-body ground states with a spectral gap property, which we call a gapped ground state, see [43]
for recent developments. In our free fermionic model with BdG Hamiltonian H, the ground state
on V∗V will be gapped if 0 /∈spec(H). If H is a gapped (invertible) BdG Hamiltonian, iH is a Real,
skew-adjoint, and invertible operator on H that anti-commutes with ungraded Clifford generators
{κ1, . . . , κn}. We can therefore describe a topological obstruction between pairs of symmetric and
gapped BdG Hamiltonians (with the same symmetry) via the DK-valued relative index.
Assumption 10.2. Let H0 and H1 be invertible BdG Hamiltonians on (H, Γ) with the same free
fermionic symmetry. We assume that there is a C∗-algebra of observables A ⊂Mult(A) ⊂B(H)
such that the ungraded Clifford generators {κ1, . . . , κn} ⊂Mult(A) and AdΓ gives a well-defined
real structure on Mult(A). Furthermore, we assume that H0 and H1 are A-comparable, meaning
that JiH0 = iH0|iH0|−1, JiH1 = iH1|iH1|−1 ∈Mult(A) with
q(JiH0) −q(JiH1)

QA < 2.
The following is then immediate by the definition of the relative index.

Assumption 10.2. Then the relative topological phase
Ind(H0, H1) = rel-ind0,n+1

then the path Ht connecting the two gapped systems will have a “topological gap closing”.

A
Problems with extending the ABS isomorphism
The Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro (ABS) isomorphism Mr,s/Mr,s+1 ∼= KOs+1−r(R) provides an elegant
description of the real K-theory of a point [4], where Mr,s denotes the Grothendieck group of
the semigroup of unitary equivalence classes of (ungraded) finite-dimensional representations of
Cℓr,s. The ABS isomorphism also gives a natural generalisation of the analytic Fredholm index to
skew-adjoint Fredholm operators on a Hilbert space that anti-commute with the generators of a
Cℓr,s-representation [6].


## Page 58


58
C. Bourne, A. Carey, K. van den Dungen, A. Rennie
The isomorphism KKR(C, A) ∼= KO0(Ar) for ungraded Real C∗-algebras A gives a description of
real K-theory in degree 0 via finitely generated and projective A-modules [36, §6, Theorem 3].
For higher degrees of real K-theory, it is tempting to try and extend the Atiyah–Bott–Shapiro
approach on Hilbert spaces to give a presentation of KOs+1−r(Ar) via finite projective A-modules
with a Clifford action. In this short section we explain that the naive approach towards such a
generalisation unfortunately fails.
Suppose that A is a unital Real C∗-algebra. Let E and F be finite projective right A-modules
with adjointable left actions φE : Cℓr,s →EndA(E), φF : Cℓr,s →EndA(F) of an ungraded Clifford
algebra Cℓr,s. We say that E and F are equivalent if the modules φE(1Cℓr,s)E and φF (1Cℓr,s)F are
Cℓr,s-equivariantly unitarily equivalent.
Definition. For a unital Real C∗-algebra A, we define Mr,s(Ar) to be the Grothendieck group of
the abelian semigroup of Cℓr,s-equivariant unitary equivalence classes of (ungraded) finite projective
Real right A-modules with a left (ungraded) Cℓr,s-action.
If A is a nonunital algebra with minimal unitization A∼, we define
eMr,s(Ar) := Ker
 q : Mr,s((Ar)∼) →Mr,s(R)

,
where q([E]) = [E/E · A].
Observe that, for Ar = R, we obtain eMr,s(R) = Mr,s(R) = Mr,s.
In analogy with the ABS
isomorphism Mr,s/Mr,s+1 ∼= KOs+1−r(R), we can then define
g
KOs+1−r(Ar) := eMr,s(Ar)/ eMr,s+1(Ar).
Unfortunately, this definition of g
KOs+1−r(Ar) does not agree with KOs+1−r(Ar) for arbitrary Real
C∗-algebras.
Example. Let r = 0 and s = 2. Then, for two anti-commuting skew-adjoint Clifford generators
f1, f2, we immediately obtain a third f3 = f1f2. So for every C∗-algebra A and every A-module X
carrying a Cℓ0,2 representation, X also carries a Cℓ0,3 representation. Hence
g
KO3(Ar) = eM0,2(Ar)/ eM0,3(Ar) = 0
for every Real C∗-algebra A.
But of course, there exist Real C∗-algebras A (e.g., A = C0(R)) with KO3(Ar) ̸= 0.
This example shows that, while the ABS picture of real K-theory is valid for the scalar algebra
R (i.e., for the K-theory of a point), it is not valid in general. As far as the authors know, the
only way to salvage the above approach is by bringing in additional information in the form of
an operator F ∈Mult(A ⊗K) such that q(F) ∈QA⊗K is invertible and anti-commutes with the
Cℓr,s-generators, as in [29, Appendices]. But this puts us in the realm of Kasparov’s KK-theory,
and so we do not obtain a ‘simpler’ picture of real K-theory via the ABS approach.

Kasparov products
The internal and external Kasparov products are bilinear and functorial
maps
KKR(A, B) × KKR(B, C) →KKR(A, C),
(x, y) 7→x ⊗B y,
KKR(A, B) × KKR(C, D) →KKR(A ˆ⊗C, B ˆ⊗D),
(w, z) 7→w ⊗z.
The group KKR(A, A) becomes a ring under the internal Kasparov product, with the identity
element given by IdA = [(A, AA, 0)]. The external Kasparov product with IdA yields a map
τA : KKR(B, C) →KKR(B ˆ⊗A, C ˆ⊗A),
x 7→x ⊗IdA .
This map allows us to define a more general pairing, referred to as the internal product over C:
KKR(A1, B1 ˆ⊗C) × KKR(C ˆ⊗A2, B2) →KKR(A1 ˆ⊗A2, B1 ˆ⊗B2),
(x, y) 7→x ⊗C y := (x ⊗IdA2) ⊗B1 ˆ⊗C ˆ⊗A2 (IdB1 ⊗y).

We review Van Daele K-theory as it was first considered in [60, 61] and then further developed
in [17, 38, 39, 42, 31, 55].
C.1
Basic definition and properties
Definition C.1. Let B be a complex C∗-algebra.
We say that B has a balanced Z2-grading
if B contains an odd self-adjoint unitary (OSU). That is, there is an odd element e satisfying
e = e∗= e−1 (in particular, B must be unital). If B has a real structure r, we also require er = e.

Basepointed DK-theory
We will occasionally consider Van Daele K-theory relative to a choice
of basepoint [60]. For a balanced graded algebra B and e ∈B an odd self-adjoint unitary, we let
Ve(B) = S
k π0
 OSU(Mk(B))

where we embed Mk(B) into Mk+1(B) via x 7→x ⊕e. Van Daele’s
K-theory group with a basepoint is defined as the Grothendieck group DKe(B) = GVe(B). If e is
homotopic in OSU(B) to −e, then Ve(B) is already an abelian group with −[x] = [−exe]. So in
this setting (which can always be achieved by passing to matrices), the Grothendieck completion
is unnecessary.
The group DKe(B) does not depend on the choice of e up to isomorphism [60, Proposition 2.12].
Furthermore, for any choice of basepoint e, DKe(B) ∼= DK(B) [17, §2.1.1], and we can pass back
and forth between the relative and basepointed description of Van Daele K-theory.

For an ungraded Real C∗-algebra A, there are isomorphisms Υr,s

We will mainly be interested in the isomorphisms of Van Daele K-theory with degree-zero and

theory groups, where we use the picture of real K-theory due to Boersema and Loring [14].

with grading Γ(α, β) = (β, α). We obtain the Real Clifford algebra Cℓ0,1 by taking Cℓ1 ∼= C ⊕C
with real structure (α, β)r0,1 = (β, α) (in particular, we have ρ ∼(i, −i)). We have that odd self-
adjoint unitaries in A ⊗Cℓ1 are of the form x = (2p −1) ⊗(1, −1). If we impose xrA⊗r0,1 = x, then
(2p−1)r = −(2p−1). In [14], Boersema and Loring characterise the group KO2(Ar) via equivalence
classes of self-adjoint unitaries such that ur = −u. In particular, the map (2p−1)⊗(1, −1) 7→2p−1
will give an isomorphism DK(A ⊗Cℓ0,1) ∼= KO2(Ar).
KR−1 and KR3
To consider DK(A ⊗Cℓ2,0), we take Cℓ2 ∼= M2(C) with the real structure
r2,0 = Adσ1 ◦c, where c denotes entrywise complex conjugation. We find that σr2,0
1

= σ2,
and Cℓr2,0
2
∼= Cℓ2,0.
Any odd element in A ⊗Cℓ2 can be written as x = a ⊗σ1 + b ⊗σ2.
If
x = x∗= x−1, then a and b are self-adjoint and u = a + ib ∈A is unitary. Imposing the condition
xr2,0 = x, we obtain that ar = a and br = b and hence ur = u∗. To summarise, odd self-adjoint
unitaries in A ⊗Cℓ2,0 can be described in terms of unitaries u ∈A such that ur = u∗. Boersema
and Loring characterise KO−1(Ar) by classes of unitaries u ∈Mn(A) such that u∗= ur.2 Hence
the map a ⊗σ1 + b ⊗σ2 7→a + ib will furnish an isomorphism DK(A ⊗Cℓ2,0) ∼= KO−1(Ar).
We can similarly consider the real structure r0,2 = Ad−iσ2 ◦c on Cℓ2 ∼= M2(C), which has Real
elements iσ1 and −iσ2, whence Cℓr0,2
2
= Cℓ0,2. So if the odd self-adjoint unitary x = a⊗σ1 +b⊗σ2
is invariant under r0,2, it follows that ar = −a and br = −b. Therefore the unitary u = a + ib is
such that ur = −u∗. We can therefore describe classes of DK(A ⊗Cℓ0,2) via unitaries in M2m(A)

To consider groups KR4 and higher, we can instead use the K¨unneth formula

KO−1(Ar ⊗H).

Isomorphisms between KKR and DK
For a σ-unital and Z2-graded Real C∗-algebra B, the isomorphism KKR(Cℓ1,0, B) ∼= DK(B) has
been established in various forms [55, 42, 17]. Here we examine some of these maps, their compat-
ibility with each other as well as other natural maps in KKR and DK-theory.
D.1
The Roe and Kubota isomorphisms
Much of the content of this section can also be found in [42, §5]. We first review the Z2-graded
isomorphisms and then consider the setting of skew-adjoint and Clifford anti-linear operators.

D.2
The Cayley isomorphism
We complete our discussion by describing an isomorphism between KKR-theory and DK-theory
via the Cayley transform studied in [17]. This isomorphism is well-adapted to computations with
unbounded Kasparov modules. We first review the isomorphism for graded algebras.
